Abstract: SIREN (Ship Inviscid Resistance and Elevation eNgine) is a Rankine-source panel solver for the steady wave resistance of a ship, in the tradition of Dawson's method. The free-surface condition is linearised about a base flow, and the linearisation is iterated. In the present formulation the base flow is the solution of a Neumann problem on the actual boundary: sources on the entire hull, wet and dry, and on the free surface panels, with impermeability imposed on both, so that the free surface acts as a rigid lid at the position it currently occupies. This replaces the classical double-body flow, whose image about z=0 is meaningful only for a flat surface and a hull cut at z=0, and it removes the need for desingularised free-surface panels. In the non-linear iteration the free-surface panels are moved rigidly in the vertical to the relaxed wave elevation at their centroid, the hull panels are marked wet or dry against the moved surface, and the base flow is recomputed on the moved geometry; impermeability applies to the whole hull regardless of this marking, which is used only to select which panels enter the force integral, and under-relaxation is confined to the passes where the non-linear correction accumulates without being reset. The radiation condition is imposed by a one-row upstream shift of the collocation points, and the wave resistance is obtained by pressure integration and, independently, by transverse wave-cut analysis.

</details>

Abstract: Extending the Fokker--Planck equation to a coupled system of equations through non-Abelian symmetry groups offers a powerful theoretical framework for coupled multi-state stochastic systems. However, translating this abstract gauge field formulation into viable numerical predictions remains an unaddressed challenge. Motivated by our previous work on ice crystal habit evolution, in this work we bridge this gap by presenting the first numerical solver for an SU(2) gauge-coupled Fokker--Planck system in (1+1) dimensions. Using an operator-splitting scheme combined with a non-oscillatory advection solver, our algorithm strictly ensures probability conservation, non-negativity, and numerical stability. We demonstrate that non-Abelian gauge coupling acts as a cross-reactive feedback loop that fundamentally alters system dynamics, driving continuous probability exchange between states, suppressing localised density peaks, and forcing long-time spatial delocalisation. Validated across linear and quadratic coupling regimes up to strong interactions (g <= 1.0), this work establishes the foundational computational infrastructure needed to integrate complex multi-state physics. Overall, while our approach provides a key step toward improving ice cloud representation in atmospheric and climate models, the underlying physical framework and computational engine remain fundamentally general and multidisciplinary, offering a versatile paradigm for any domain requiring coupled multi-state stochastic equations.

</details>

Abstract: acousticHelmholtzFoam is an OpenFOAM application that computes time-harmonic acoustic pressure fields in heterogeneous gas-liquid media on unstructured polyhedral meshes. The complex pressure is stored as real and imaginary volume fields, the material coefficients are constructed from a volume-of-fluid phase fraction with geometric face-fraction averaging, and open boundaries are truncated by Cartesian perfectly matched layers (PMLs) whose damping tensor is generated by the companion utility setPMLFields. The two coupled real-valued equations are discretized with the cell-centred finite-volume operators of OpenFOAM and assembled explicitly, including processor couplings and boundary contributions, into a distributed PETSc matrix that is solved by default with the MUMPS sparse direct solver. The solver writes pressure amplitude, complex velocity, acoustic radiation pressure and time-averaged momentum flux for acoustic-force evaluation. This paper documents the implemented equations, the case interface, the assembly and solution algorithm, the build procedure and the test infrastructure. A layered gas-liquid-PML case with an analytical solution serves as an end-to-end regression: on 8000 cells the whole-domain relative complex-pressure error is $1.197\times10^{-3}$, and serial and MPI solutions on up to eight ranks agree to within $10^{-12}$ in relative $L_2$ norm. A single-axis acoustic-levitator height sweep with constant-volume spheroidal drops demonstrates the two-phase material input and the force output, and strong- and problem-size-scaling measurements characterize the direct-solver workflow. The numerical formulation and its verification are presented in a companion paper (arXiv:2610.01593).

</details>

Abstract: The sudden growth in AI data centers is raising numerous environmental concerns. Low-frequency noise pollution is a concern for local residents, that may not just be an annoyance but also carry significant health impacts. An FDTD model of sound propagation over the topography near an AI data center is presented. The topography plays a large role in the level of noise pollution, with areas of sudden elevation on either side of the river near the AI data center more susceptible to high noise levels, while areas further afield largely protected from noise pollution by the topography. Understanding the role that topography plays in noise pollution, and capturing the noise pollution in specific topographies, can help identify areas where noise mitigation strategies might be best employed.

</details>

Abstract: We present a mass-weighted meshless formulation for the shifted incompressible lattice Boltzmann method (LBM) whose streaming step is exactly unitary, combining with the Carleman embedding. In traditional meshless LBM, on a scattered node set the semi-Lagrangian streaming step is an interpolation, which is not unitary; a generic block encoding of this matrix therefore requires a normalization proportional to its sparsity and logarithmically many ancillas. To overcome the non-unitarity, we instead express the interpolation as the exponential exp(a Dx + b Dy) of mimetic derivative operators. Endowing each node with its Voronoi mass and applying a mass-weighted similarity transform renders these derivative operators skew-Hermitian by enforcing moment constraints together with low-wave number trigonometric differentiation conditions, so that the interpolation becomes exactly unitary. Such unitarization of interpolation leave the local collision step unchanged up to a rescaling of its quadratic term, where the same weights define the amplitude encoding of the distribution functions. For quasi-uniform node sets, the mass-weighting diagonal remains within a unit-order normalization that degrades only mildly as the Carleman truncation order grows, thereby mitigating the cost of block encoding. Numerical experiments on the Taylor Green vortex confirm that the method reproduces the full meshless LBM, with the velocity error decreasing as the Carleman order increases.

</details>

Abstract: Projection-based model-order reduction (MOR) offers a promising approach for accelerating expensive full-order model (FOM) by constructing reduced-order models (ROMs) that retain the majority of FOM accuracy while significantly reducing the computational cost. However, conventional static ROMs, often following offline/online training strategy, can exhibit limited predictive capability for convection-dominated problems featuring strongly evolving physics such as shocks. Adaptive ROMs emerge as a promising alternative and address such limitation by dynamically updating the low-dimensional subspace during online simulation to track locally evolving system dynamics, thereby enabling accurate predictions even for shock-dominated flows. However, such online adaptation introduces additional computational overhead, and frequent basis and sampling-point updates can substantially reduce their computational advantage relative to their static counterpart. To reconcile this tradeoff, the current work introduces a self-adaptive MOR framework that dynamically constructs and deploys the most appropriate ROM formulation (either adaptive or static) on the fly according to the evolving characteristics of the solution. to achieve optimal computational efficiency while maintaining high modeling accuracy of the local physics during the online model execution. In this regard, the proposed framework seeks to exploit the advantages of both adaptive- and static-ROM approaches to achieve an optimal computational efficiency and predictive accuracy while reducing reliance on extensive offline training. The framework is developed and evaluated for modeling rotating detonation engines (RDEs), which provide a challenging test environment involving strongly convection-dominated, transient, and multiscale reacting-flow dynamics.

</details>

Abstract: The use of artificial intelligence (AI) in physics research is increasingly common, yet few guidelines exist for its effective use. Starting with a principles-based approach, we outline a set of ten requirements for collaborative AI agents in experimental physics, spanning scientific norms, effective agent use, and domain expertise. We take a human-centered approach in which agents act as collaborators that amplify rather than replace the expertise of a human researcher (user) who directs the work and is responsible for its results. We then present a framework designed to enforce these requirements, detailing methods for improving off-the-shelf behavior of large language models (LLMs) with custom scaffolding and describing general-purpose scientific workflows and skills. In particular, we highlight the power of closed-loop campaigns where agents work iteratively toward a well-defined goal. We demonstrate the potential of this mode of research through six case studies in experimental physics, covering device design, hardware control, neural network optimization, physical model identification, and data analysis. All six case studies result in useful work products that add utility to our research, including a neural network architecture and solid-state quantum defect photodynamic model that substantially outperform our published work; a microwave waveguide design that extends a published design concept to a fabrication-ready device meeting our experimental constraints; a quantum control pulse sequence candidate that improves simulated performance by more than an order of magnitude; commissioning of an NMR spectrometer to $\sim$1~Hz frequency precision on live hardware; and finally a data-fitting pipeline that achieves, to our knowledge, the first reconstruction of the Ising microstate of an artificial spin-ice array from diffraction-limited magnetic imaging.

</details>

Abstract: General-purpose molecular dynamics software supports diverse interatomic potentials and simulation requirements. Within a simulation stage, the loaded potential model and the required calculations typically remain fixed as the atomic configuration evolves. We present the Wisevolve Potential Engine (WPE), which uses this information from GPUMD to select and reuse a GPU execution policy tailored to the model and simulation stage. GPUMD retains simulation control and ownership of the simulation data. On a single NVIDIA RTX 5090, WPE-enabled GPUMD (GPUMD-WPE) achieves a median throughput of 109 million atom-step/s across 106 neuroevolution potential (NEP) workloads. For a 15.1-million-atom diamond Si system, it sustains 142 million atom-step/s. Relative to GPUMD, geometric-mean speedups are 3.49x across these NEP workloads, 3.61x across 16 workloads using NEP with dynamic charges (qNEP), 1.24x across 28 classical-potential workloads, and 10.5x across 56 path-integral molecular dynamics (PIMD) configurations. End-to-end calculations of water structure, lattice thermal conductivity, and PIMD thermal expansion show close agreement with corresponding GPUMD results and published simulation results. A separate 12.5-million-atom Cu shock-compression simulation demonstrates its applicability to large-scale nonequilibrium dynamics. These results support model- and stage-specific specialization as a practical way to accelerate GPU potential evaluation in general-purpose molecular dynamics workflows.

</details>

Abstract: Continuum gyrokinetic codes use structured, field-aligned grids on nested flux surfaces; particle-in-cell codes support unstructured meshes and open field lines but carry a statistical noise floor. We present a continuum drift-kinetic solver that occupies neither position: a semi-Lagrangian discontinuous Galerkin discretization on unstructured triangular poloidal planes extruded toroidally, whose parallel advance is a map along the exact field line in the flux-coordinate independent manner, so the mesh is never aligned with the field, with adiabatic electrons and a long-wavelength polarization field solve. Every operator is verified against an independently computed reference, from Landau damping to the geodesic-acoustic root and a linear ion-temperature-gradient eigenvalue problem, every measured rate within 4% except the geodesic-acoustic damping, within 12%. A linear toroidal ion-temperature-gradient demonstration on a canonical-Maxwellian equilibrium recovers a radially coherent mode whose growth rate, 0.065 c_s/a, is stationary in time. The collisionless zonal-flow residual is the one measurement still moving: it rises with poloidal refinement from 0.72 to 0.87 of the model's own orbit-quadrature prediction for the measured observable and has not converged. The solver is fully device resident, and every result is reproduced between CPU and GPU builds and across MPI process counts. The code, the data behind every table and an artifact description are in a public repository.

</details>

Abstract: Real-space interference of wave packets is governed by their central momentum separation and momentum width, which set the oscillation wavelength and spatial envelope, respectively. Here, we show that in crystalline systems, quantum metric distribution in an isolated band can similarly give rise to real-space interference through the evanescent tails of a localized bound state. The location and width of a quantum metric hot spot in the momentum space set a complex wavevector as one evanescent plane wave component of a bound state. Consequently, with multiple quantum metric hot spots, the density distribution of a bound state or the total density of two spatially overlapped bound states exhibits an interference pattern, where the oscillation period and long-range decay length are determined by the momentum-space separation of the hot spots and the width of the narrowest hot spots, respectively. This quantum-geometric interference is particularly pronounced in (nearly) flat bands, where competing scales from band dispersion are suppressed. Furthermore, we demonstrate that using a Josephson STM the real-space interference can be experimentally observed through the tip-position dependence of the Josephson critical current. Our results reveal a direct correspondence between momentum-space quantum geometry and real-space interference, showing how bound-state profiles encode the momentum-resolved quantum metric.

</details>

Abstract: The nuclear spin of a phosphorus atom in silicon is considered as a promising quantum information carrier for quantum computing due to its long coherence time. Coherent control of the nuclear spin is typically realized using nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR). In this work, we theoretically propose that with the aid of hyperfine interactions and magnetic field gradients, fast electric-dipole-induced nuclear electric resonance (EDNER) can be achieved. The stronger EDNER (compared with NMR) arises from the combined effects of hyperfine interaction between the nuclear and electron spins and coupling between the electron spin and charge degrees of freedom mediated by magnetic field gradients in this scheme. By operating at specific working points, the nuclear-spin splitting becomes less sensitive to electrical noise, enabling long coherence times and high-fidelity quantum operations. All elctrical, addressable control of nuclear spins paves the way for scalable silicon-based quantum computing.

</details>

Abstract: In electronic devices, the quality of the dielectric material is determined by figures of merit such as the band gap, leakage onset field, breakdown field and the number of built-in defects, which can all limit device performance. An additional and important metric to consider is the strength of the dielectric constant. Hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) is the benchmark gate dielectric for two-dimensional van der Waals heterostructures, owing to its wide bandgap, and atomically flat interface. At the same time, its dielectric constant (2--4) highlights the opportunity to explore alternative higher-permittivity van der Waals dielectrics that provide stronger electrostatic control. Previous work established lanthanum oxybromide (LaOBr), a layered dielectric with a predicted dielectric constant of 12.5, as a potential dielectric for van der Waals heterostructures. Using graphene field-effect devices with LaOBr, we show that the leakage-onset and breakdown fields decrease systematically with thickness. Dual-gate capacitance measurements combined with a dead-layer model reveal a bulk dielectric constant of $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bulk}} = 12.2$, in agreement with theory, while interfacial dead-layers cause the effective dielectric constant to vary from 6 to 11 across multiple thicknesses. Benchmarked against established gate dielectrics, LaOBr combines breakdown fields comparable to hBN with dielectric constants 2 to 3 times larger. By establishing the operating regime of devices with various thicknesses, we further confirm the advantage of LaOBr over hBN for high-capacitance gate-dielectric operation. These results validate LaOBr as a promising dielectric for integration into two-dimensional heterostructures, and establish thickness-dependent benchmarking as essential for evaluating emerging layered dielectric materials.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum geometric quantities, such as Berry curvature (BC) and quantum metric, are known to govern the physical and topological properties of materials, making access to these quantities critically important for the comprehensive understanding of the materials. Chiral crystals offer a natural platform in which structural handedness can control quantum geometry and nonlinear transport, but direct experimental access to the quantum geometric quantities has remained elusive. Here, we show that trigonal tellurium (Te) hosts an enantiomer-dependent Berry curvature dipole (BCD) that can be accessed through the measurement of cross-axial nonlinear Hall effect (NHE), unlike previously studied in-plane configurations where the BCD contribution vanishes. Angle-resolved measurements on left- and right-handed Te devices reveal a second-order transverse response whose sign reverses with crystal chirality. The extracted cross-axial nonlinear susceptibility, $χ_{yxz}$, exceeds the in-plane susceptibility, $χ_{xxx}$, by more than two-folds, indicating a strong BCD-driven contribution. First-principles calculations attribute this behavior to a topologically nontrivial hedgehog-like BC texture near the H points and predict a BCD that reverses sign between opposite enantiomers, consistent with experiment. These results establish cross-axial nonlinear Hall transport as a symmetry-selective probe of chirality-controlled quantum geometry in tellurium and related chiral materials.

</details>

Abstract: Indirect excitons (IXs) in a periodic moiré potential in a van der Waals heterostructure form an experimental platform for exploring Bose-Hubbard physics, including the superfluid state and the Mott insulator state. The realization of these collective states requires low-disorder materials. In particular, the localization due to the Mott insulator must be distinguished from disorder-induced localization. We studied the transport kinetics of IXs in a MoSe$_2$/WSe$_2$ van der Waals heterostructure with an anomalously high IX mobility, consistent with superfluidity. This high-mobility transport demonstrates that disorder-induced localization is negligible. We found a remarkable suppression of this efficient IX transport, directly indicating the Mott insulator. As the IX density increases during the excitation pulse, the IX cloud initially expands and then contracts upon entering the Mott insulator phase. The emergence of IX cloud contraction is accompanied by a peak in the IX spatial distribution at the origin. Our simulations reproduce the observed non-monotonic IX transport kinetics, including the temporal expansion and contraction of the IX cloud, as well as the peak in its spatial distribution.

</details>

Abstract: By considering the energy transport of spin wave, we show that, like the electron Klein paradox, there is no magnon Klein paradox for reflectivity. Heuristically, this can be understood according to the fact that, even if the ground state is unstable, any energy-carrying wave cannot have greater-than-one reflectivity off a passive interface, which will violate the conservation of energy. The magnon Klein paradox discussed in the literature is derived from assigning a wrong sign to the transmitted spin wave.

Abstract: Low-energy electrons in graphene obey an emergent, rotationally invariant Dirac equation, while the honeycomb lattice enters only through higher-order, symmetry-breaking corrections, the leading one being trigonal warping. This provides a unique solid-state arena to address a cornerstone challenge in quantum gravity: how to detect ultraviolet symmetry-breaking granular corrections, suppressed by a fundamental scale, atop an emergent infrared relativistic continuum. We show that intense counter-rotating $ω$-$2ω$ fields and valley-resolved high-harmonic generation (HHG) provide such a probe. Within a family of models that interpolates continuously between the isotropic Dirac cone and nearest-neighbour graphene at fixed Fermi velocity, the valley contrast of the harmonic yield vanishes identically in the symmetric theory, grows linearly with the warping coupling, and its valley-even counterpart appears only at second order, as expected from effective-field-theory power counting. The stronger the laser, the farther it drives electrons from the Dirac points, so the laser intensity plays the role of the collision energy. For the fourth harmonic, the leading warping correction alone accounts for the valley contrast of real graphene up to fundamental intensities of about $10^{11}$ W/cm$^2$, below the damage threshold of monolayer graphene, whereas the fifth harmonic already reveals higher-order lattice corrections at intensities around $10^{10}$ W/cm$^2$. The two harmonics, which rotate in opposite directions, are emitted by opposite valleys. Valley-resolved harmonics are far more sensitive than the residual valley populations, making them a practical detector of lattice-scale physics hidden beneath an emergent Dirac theory.

</details>

Abstract: Recent experiments highlighted the essential role of the orbital degrees of freedom underlying current-induced magnetic dynamics. However, a microscopic picture of how orbital angular momenta are transferred between itinerant and localized electrons, and the way it feeds back into transport, remains elusive. Here we develop a theoretical framework for the orbital exchange interactions by generalizing the Anderson-Kondo model to incorporate the orbitals of conduction bands. Illustrating with the $p$-orbitals, we determine the way electrons acquire orbital polarization and its influence on the transport properties, uncovering the orbital counterparts of phenomena analogous to spin filtering and tunnel magnetoresistance. Reciprocally, we demonstrate that orbital-polarized currents can exert torques on localized magnetic moments without having to convert into spin currents, which enables electrical switching of magnetization even when spin polarization becomes inactive. These striking discoveries lay a microscopic foundation for orbitronics and reveal critical mechanisms for orbital manipulations of magnetic order.

Abstract: A solvable microscopic model of interacting electrons in a half-filled Landau level is presented. The Hamiltonian is a well-defined limit of the electron-electron interaction. The low energy sector consists of a set of independent, spatially interlaced, one-dimensional systems of non-interacting fermionic dipoles, each system with a Fermi sea ground state. The total number of dipoles in the set of Fermi seas forming the two-dimensional ground state equals the number of electrons in the system and the density of states at the Fermi level is that of a two-dimensional Fermi gas. The model is obtained by generalizing, or rather adopting, the existing exact solution on a thin, but not too thin, torus to the two-dimensional bulk case.

</details>

Abstract: The integer and fractional quantum Hall effects reveal that quantized physical responses can take integer and fractional values, respectively. This naturally raises the question of whether quantization can extend beyond rational numbers to an irrational value. Here we show that such a quantized response can emerge from the limiting behavior of a finite, experimentally realizable geometric network. We develop a method based on infinite geometric structures, in which a sequence of finite networks rapidly converges toward an infinite limit. Following this approach, we identify a planar network whose normalized resistance converges to the irrational value $\sqrt{3}$, and realize a finite approximation with high-precision geometric control. The measured normalized resistance is $1.735 \pm 0.001\, h/e^2$ for a fully occupied Landau level in the designed pattern. Our experimental results extend the concept of quantization beyond rational values and demonstrate that an irrational quantized response can be approached through a finite physical system.

</details>

Yet the wave-vector range between roughly 30 and 300 radians per micrometre has remained difficult to access experimentally, posing challenges for both excitation and detection. This Perspective reviews recent advances in emerging measurement techniques and discusses prospects for their future development. Optical and microwave techniques are restricted to the vicinity of the Brillouin-zone centre. Inelastic neutron, electron and resonant hard x-ray scattering cover the full Brillouin zone but lack the energy resolution needed for low-frequency magnons at intermediate wave vectors. This Perspective examines five emerging approaches that are beginning to close this gap: Mie-enhanced Brillouin light scattering, colour-centre magnetometry, magnon momentum microscopy, extreme-ultraviolet transient-grating spectroscopy, and time-resolved scanning transmission x-ray microscopy. For each, we discuss the physical principle, demonstrated capabilities, current limitations and prospects for development. Compared side by side, the techniques give complementary access to magnon frequencies, local stray fields, real-space dynamics and momentum-space distributions, and they differ in their sensitivity to coherent and incoherent excitations. We therefore discuss the main advantages and use cases of each technique and consider the practical requirements for addressing different scientific questions. We highlight opportunities for further development, including higher sensitivity, improved spatial and temporal resolution, and broader frequency and wave-vector coverage. Together, these advances could enable, for example, systematic studies of nonlinear magnon interactions at nanoscale wavelengths and investigations of emerging magnetic materials such as altermagnets.

Abstract: Flat bands amplify interactions into correlated phases, and topological bound states are robust by principle; combining the two in a model that is both exactly analyzable and experimentally accessible is therefore especially desirable. Here we show that a square-lattice model with a slowly modulated Dirac mass texture, $m(x)=m+m_0\cos(2πx/L)$ with $L$ up to hundreds of sites, provides such a setting, without engineered geometries, lattice-scale textures, or long-range hoppings. Exact nesting reduces the problem, independently at each transverse momentum $k_y$, to a $1+1$ dimensional Dirac Hamiltonian with a position-dependent mass: every simple zero of $m(x)$ binds Jackiw-Rebbi zero modes, and the two walls of a period form an effective Su-Schrieffer-Heeger ring. The model interpolates between a chirally pinned flat band at large $L$, a helical miniband whose nodes are enforced by a boundary phase of the ring, and a bias-dimerized regime in which the sign of the bias sets a winding number. A central result is that the miniband bandwidth is not a single scale: a max-min measurement conflates three widths of different origin, the helical height $W_{\rm JR}$ exponential in the period, the zone-edge upturn $W_{\rm edge}\propto L^{-2/3}$ dominating at large $L$, and a bias bump exponential in $L$, so the flat band is robust against bias in the thermodynamic limit. We analyze two lattice realizations, a two-orbital model with modulated inter-orbital hybridization and a single-orbital bond-order texture sharing the nodal physics while leaving the van Hove points gapless, and propose experiments on cold-atom superlattices, Kekule-ordered graphene, STM designer lattices, and microwave arrays.

Abstract: The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in unconventional magnets has attracted considerable attention. A giant AHE has recently been observed in the candidate $p$-wave magnet Gd$_3$(Ru$_{1-δ}$Rh$_δ$)$_4$Al$_{12}$ [Nature 646, 837(2025)], while its theoretical interpretation invokes a spontaneous magnetization to break time-reversal symmetry ($\mathcal{T}$), although the magnetic order itself already breaks $\mathcal{T}$. Consequently, the microscopic origin of the AHE in odd-parity magnets (OPMs) remains an open question. Here we establish the symmetry condition and microscopic mechanism for the AHE in OPMs. Since the OPM order is classified by the spin group whereas the AHE is governed by the magnetic group, the AHE is allowed only when the magnetic point group is ferromagnetic; conversely, it is forbidden whenever the effective time reversal $\mathcal{T}τ$ (with $τ$ a fractional translation), which is essential to the realization of the OPM, is preserved. Microscopically, OPMs generically host a hidden Zeeman field (HZF) rooted in this intrinsic $\mathcal{T}$ breaking, and its interplay with spin-orbit coupling (SOC) generates the AHE without any external field. We demonstrate this picture with models on the square, honeycomb, and kagome lattices. In particular, a square-lattice helimagnet with sublattice-resolved SOC maps exactly onto an anisotropic Rashba--Zeeman model, whose Zeeman term is nothing but the HZF and directly yields the AHE. Our results establish the symmetry condition for the AHE in OPMs and identify the HZF--SOC interplay as a general route to realize it.

Abstract: Coherently driven phonons are conventionally described classically. Nonlinear phonon-phonon interactions, however, allow an infrared drive to reshape the fluctuation spectrum and generate substantial nonthermal enhancement of phonon fluctuations, motivating a description beyond the coherent displacement alone. Abstract: The frequency noise of a resonant nanomechanical sensor is well understood down to its thermomechanical limit, but the longest useful averaging time is set by a flicker, or $1/f$, frequency-noise floor whose origin has remained unknown. We show that this floor is the thermal noise of the tension. By the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, the tension of a resonator with fixed ends fluctuates by an amount set by the loss angle of its material; for structural damping, these fluctuations produce $1/f$ frequency noise with a flicker coefficient $h_{-1}=E k_{\mathrm{B}} T/(2πVσ_0^2 Q_{\mathrm{el}})$, with $Q_{\mathrm{el}}$ the intrinsic loss under elongation, that contains no free parameter and is diluted by the tensile stress $σ_0$ in the same way as damping is in dissipation dilution. Silicon nitride strings and membranes from 30 to 340~nm thickness follow the predicted volume scaling, the predicted $σ_0^{-2}$ dilution across resonators and within stress-engineered strings, and the predicted thickness dependence, which places noise-active two-level systems in the volume and at the surfaces of the resonators, all at the absolute level set by the independently measured intrinsic loss. The same relation accounts for published data between 78 and 296~K; below 78~K, the loss measured at the resonance no longer represents the loss probed by the noise, marking the limit of the structural-damping assumption. Dissipation dilution therefore lowers the $1/f$ floor, and the longest useful integration time of a resonant sensor is set by its prestress, volume, temperature, and intrinsic loss angle.

</details>

Abstract: We reconstruct a geometry of thermodynamic coordinates from transport coefficients within a mesoscopic canonical model. Self-adjoint flux observables determine a compatible evolution generator containing symmetric and antisymmetric transport coefficients. When the Hermitian transport tensor is positive, an exact graded first-order factorization reproduces this generator on its scalar sector. Its commutators define a spectral distance whose metric depends only on the symmetric tensor. Gradients of the antisymmetric tensor can change the evolution while leaving the metric unchanged. The construction allows coordinate-dependent coefficients. In the constant Onsager regime, a Clifford representation gives the same distance and an algebra fixed by the transport matrix. Published transport data for a $\mathrm{Bi_2Te_3}$ film give a principal-distance ratio of approximately $2.12$ in normalized coordinates at $ZT\simeq0.68$. The spinorial representation adds no physical spin degrees of freedom.

Abstract: Direct and parametric driving together generate nonlinear response structures beyond conventional Duffing hysteresis, including multiple switching regions and isolated branches. Understanding how these structures are connected requires identifying the bifurcations that reorganize the response as the parametric excitation is varied. At a phase difference of 45 degrees between the direct and parametric excitations, we experimentally and analytically determine how the steady-state response changes as the parametric excitation is increased. A single resonance peak first splits into two while the response remains single valued. Two successive cusp bifurcations then create two saddle-node pairs and establish a double-hysteretic response. At the parametric-instability threshold, one saddle node of each pair is displaced towards unbounded amplitude and detuning, and an isolated-branch structure emerges above threshold. Measurements on a dielectrically actuated pre-stressed SiN nanostring reproduce the predicted response and associated switching behavior. These results establish the bifurcation pathway connecting the different response structures at this phase configuration, providing a systematic basis for controlling multistability and switching in parametrically driven nonlinear resonators.

Abstract: Structured light provides a powerful means of controlling matter while itself exhibiting nontrivial spatial topology. Our results show how qubit dynamics can be used to probe Majorana protection in experimentally realistic devices.

Abstract: Anyons are particles with quantized fractional statistics. However, experiments to date have focused on probing anyons in structures in which many anyonic excitations coexist and interact. Here, we demonstrate the trapping of a single anyon in a device consisting of a graphene-based quantum point contact with an embedded tunable quantum Hall dot. Benchmarking our device at v = 1, we demonstrate backscattering between Fermi liquid edge states mediated by the whole-electron Coulomb blockade of the dot. The cleanliness and control available in our device allow depletion of the dot to the last quasihole, and we observe spin blockade for transitions to the opposite-spin Landau level. At v = 1/3 and 2/3, we observe backscattering resonances with three times the frequency, consistent with resonant transmission of e/3 charges via a single anyon transistor. We demonstrate controlled depletion to the last quasiparticle to form a single anyon quantum dot. Tracing the charge addition spectrum for quasiparticle number Nqp = 1-5, we observe highly irregular addition energies, consistent with DMRG modeling showing a complex orbital shell-filling behavior. At higher Nqp = 10-20, the addition energies show a striking pattern: quasiholes at 1/3 (and quasiparticles at 2/3) show a regular addition spectrum while quasiparticles at 1/3 (and quasiholes at 2/3) do not. We trace the origin of this irregularity to an attractive component of the inter-anyon interaction: by experimentally tuning the confining energy, we observe complete suppression of alternating charge sectors, after which the dot charges in increments of twice the fundamental charge. This observation indicates the emergence of an anyon binding energy as large as the charging energy.

Abstract: We show how to perform exact diagonalization of SU($N$)-symmetric Bose-Hubbard models separately in each irreducible representation of SU($N$), by exploiting the Howe duality between U($L$) and U($N$). Three structural consequences follow for any lattice and any Hamiltonian built from the U($L$) generators: (i) the spectrum in a given orbital sector is independent of $N$ and of the particle statistics, (ii) the canonical partition functions for bosons and fermions obey the exact identity $Z_{\mathrm{b}}(-N)=(-1)^{N_\mathrm{b}}Z_{\mathrm{f}}(N)$, (iii) with unfrustrated hopping the groundstate is always a color ferromagnet, so that the color-resolved method we introduce is useful to study excited sectors, frustrated lattices and finite temperatures. As applications, we first derive the finite-temperature phase diagram of attractive SU($N$) bosons on few-site rings at large $N$ for arbitrary filling. We then show the emergence of singlet-cluster states from frustrated hopping, the counterpart of fermionic ferromagnetism, with the example of the sawtooth and kagome lattice.

Abstract: Fermi surface transformation accompanied by quantum phase transition is widely observed in correlated electron systems including heavy fermion materials and van der Waals quantum heterostructures. The unified Anderson lattice description for this phenomenon suggests that their interacting ground states in the case of electron half filling always destabilize the metallic phase in the strong Kondo-coupling regime. In the weak Kondo-coupling regime, the system exhibits a fractional Dirac liquid involving the massless or massive Dirac fermions and a charge neutral spinon Fermi surface. In the strong Kondo-coupling regime, the spinons hybridize with the Dirac fermions resulting in an intermediate nearly-flat hybridizing band together with the conduction and valence heavy fermion bands, yielding a large electric Fermi surface. Absence of the Kondo gap between these bands is observed depending on the stacking configurations of the layered structure. In particular, the system remains metallic even at the exact half filling in this regime and Luttinger's theorem is violated across the critical point. Implications of these results on the van der Waals quantum heterostructures are briefly discussed.

Abstract: Bloch oscillations typically arise in periodic systems subjected to a constant force generated by a potential gradient. Here, we study domain-wall dynamics in coupled Ising layers under asymmetric longitudinal fields. The interlayer coupling is of Heisenberg type and acts as an effective transverse coupling in the emergent pseudospin representation. We show that the rung pseudospin structure gives rise to kinetic constraints, restricting the many-body dynamics to an emergent one-dimensional quasiparticle subspace within the original two-dimensional system. Abstract: The Unified Field Bosonization Technique (UFBT) is employed to investigate the single-particle properties of inhomogeneous Luttinger liquids containing localized clusters of impurities in the presence of forward-scattering interactions. The results reveal distinct power-law behaviours of the DDOS near the impurity cluster and in the far-field regime. The tunneling conductance exhibits a power-law temperature dependence, decreasing with decreasing temperature for repulsive interactions and increasing for attractive interactions, while remaining temperature-independent in the noninteracting limit. The impurity cluster modifies the amplitudes and spatial structure of these observables, whereas their characteristic power-law exponents are determined solely by the interaction parameters. The formulation accommodates arbitrary impurity strengths without requiring perturbative expansions in the impurity or interaction strengths. The characteristic scaling behaviour emerges directly from the fermionic correlation functions, without an explicit renormalization-group (RG) treatment of the impurity strength, while the weak-interaction limit recovers the leading perturbative correction to the tunneling response. These findings establish UFBT as a unified framework for describing local spectral properties and cross-barrier transport in inhomogeneous Luttinger liquids.

Abstract: Magnetic circular dichroism (MCD) is an optical probe of orbital time reversal symmetry breaking. Although frequency integrated MCD is related to the self-rotation contribution to orbital magnetization at zero field, its magnetic response generally differs from the thermodynamic orbital susceptibility. Here, we define the MCD susceptibility and derive general expressions for multiband systems using an electromagnetic $U(1)$ gauge invariant approach. For insulators, the response contains contributions from the non-Abelian quantum metric, Berry curvature, and field induced interband mixing. For metals, a finite temperature Green function formulation separates the smooth response into Fermi sea and Fermi surface contributions. An independent Landau level calculation confirms the expressions for the zero-field MCD moment and its linear magnetic response. In a continuum model for twisted bilayer graphene, the MCD susceptibility vanishes within the charge neutrality gap, while the thermodynamic orbital susceptibility remains markedly diamagnetic. Near the gap edges, the orbital moment term dominates the response. Features near the van Hove energies are associated with density of states enhancement. These results clarify how the two susceptibilities reflect different orbital processes even for the same electronic structure and thus establish the MCD susceptibility as a distinct probe of orbital magnetic response.

Abstract: Strongly interacting fermionic chains obeying a $Z_n$ symmetry can host topological phases with highly degenerate ground-state manifolds. The different ground states are connected by strongly bound fractionalized edge modes called parafermionic zero modes. While exactly preserved under time evolution at zero temperature, interactions lead to a destabilization of these modes at finite energy densities, and, given a generic Hamiltonian, obtaining the lifetime of these modes is often impossible as it requires knowledge of the full spectrum of the Hamiltonian or long time evolutions. Here, we propose a novel quantum-inspired algorithm that can be used to variationally search for these almost strong zero modes in generic parafermionic chain models. We demonstrate analytically and numerically that our ansatz faithfully reproduces the exact results and finds strongly bound parafermionic operators in the non-integrable Kitaev-Hubbard chain and chiral Potts model. Abstract: The Shannon-Rényi (SR) entropy characterizes the probability distribution of many-body wave functions in a chosen basis and is directly accessible from single-shot measurement snapshots in cold-atom experiments, yet its theoretical evaluation in interacting systems carries exponential cost. Here we construct a field-theoretic spacetime manifold for the SR entropy in which $q$ replicas are glued at the imaginary-time boundaries, recasting it as a ratio of generalized partition functions amenable to large-scale quantum Monte Carlo simulation. On this basis we develop a reweight-annealing framework with two complementary strategies: a virtual-parameter scheme interpolating between unglued and fully glued limits via binomial-weighted partial gluing, and a real-parameter scheme using a physical coupling constant as the annealing variable within stochastic series expansion. Both reduce the computational cost from exponential to polynomial. Most importantly, we establish for the first time a finite-size scaling theory for the SR entropy near a quantum critical point, $S_q=a(g)L^2+S_q^0\left((g-g_c)L^{1/ν}\right)$; data collapse of its first derivative yields the correlation-length exponent $ν$ for both the O(3) and Ising universality classes, realized in the spin-$\frac{1}{2}$ columnar dimerized Heisenberg model and the bilayer Heisenberg-Ising model. The same simulations extract the Goldstone-mode count $N_G=2$ and the $Z_2$ ground-state degeneracy $\ln 2$ from subleading terms. These results elevate the SR entropy to a standalone, order-parameter-free diagnostic of quantum criticality with the same scaling status as the entanglement entropy, and close the loop between theory and quantum simulation by enabling quantitative benchmarking of experimental snapshots from state-of-the-art quantum simulators.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum spin liquids are exotic states of matter in which strongly interacting spins remain disordered down to zero temperature and host fractionalized excitations. This has motivated an extensive search for their realization in materials. Among candidate systems, $α$-RuCl$_3$ has drawn particular attention as a platform for the quantum spin liquid proposed by Kitaev, in which the low-energy excitations in applied magnetic fields are interpreted as itinerant Majorana fermions and local flux excitations. However, conflicting experimental signatures have cast doubt on whether a Kitaev quantum spin liquid-like state is really the right starting point to interpret the low-energy states in $α$-RuCl$_3$, motivating a closer look at the nature of these excitations. Here we report an extensive high field study of specific heat up to 35 T on $α$-RuCl$_3$. We find that the low-energy thermodynamics exhibit universal $B/T$ scaling with a single effective energy scale proportional to the applied field as long as the magnetic field is not close to the $c$-axis. This behavior persists continuously from the antiferromagnetic phase boundary to the highest fields, suggesting that the perturbative Kitaev limit, with its Majorana and flux excitations, may not be an appropriate starting point for describing the low-energy thermodynamics of $α$-RuCl$_3$ for fields away from the $c$-axis.

Abstract: Quantum skyrmions in frustrated spin-$1/2$ magnets are bound states of a few flipped spins in a ferromagnetic background. The two constituents keep their identity ($7+7$ flipped spins) and are coupled by a coherent exchange of magnons, visible as number fluctuations between the two halves of the domain in excess of those of two independent skyrmions and as in-plane spin correlations between the two skyrmion walls that reach $0.16$ across the gap. These correlations follow the vortex pattern of the two textures and lock their relative helicity, the quantum counterpart of the helicity-dependent interaction of classical skyrmions. We discuss the molecule as a self-assembled two-qubit element for skyrmion helicity qubits in a single layer.

Abstract: We develop a bootstrap approach to deriving lower bounds on the spectral gap of the first Haldane pseudopotential Hamiltonian on the sphere with $N_Φ$ flux quanta for arbitrary $N_Φ$, including in the thermodynamic limit $N_Φ\to \infty$. Our approach can in principle be generalized to pseudopotentials describing other model wavefunctions such as the Moore-Read state or Read-Rezayi sequence. Our approach is based on Knabe's observation that if $H$ is a Hamiltonian with ground state energy $0$ then the spectral gap is at least $ε> 0$ if and only if $H^2 \succeq εH$. To show this inequality, we split $H^2$ into two parts, one that can be analytically bounded and another that can be bounded using a finite semidefinite program (SDP). Crucially, the size of this program is determined only by cutoff parameters, and the program depends on $N_Φ$ only parametrically, allowing us to obtain numerically exact (up to solver precision) lower bounds on the gap. In order to turn these numerical bounds into mathematically rigorous bounds, we introduce an algorithm that can produce nearly-optimal exact feasible points for large rational SDPs. We additionally provide an algorithm that establishes the rank stability of the semidefinite program in the limit $N_Φ\to \infty$ which is needed to certify the bound in the thermodynamic limit. Beyond establishing the Laughlin gap and revealing a potential method for deriving spectral gap lower bounds for other pseudopotentials, our work provides a general method for turning numerical semidefinite programming bounds into rigorous mathematical proofs, which may have wide applicability.

</details>

Abstract: Magnetoelectric (ME) effects in antiferromagnets provide a fertile platform for exploring symmetry-driven cross-correlated responses. However, their microscopic origin remains elusive and is often obscured in simplified low-energy descriptions. In this study, we revisit the microscopic mechanism of the ME effect in a collinear antiferromagnetic zigzag chain by employing a multi-orbital tight-binding model that explicitly includes both $s$- and $p$-orbital degrees of freedom. Using analytical and numerical calculations based on the Kubo formula, we demonstrate that the ME response is governed by orbital degrees of freedom activated through $s$--$p$ hybridization, while the spin contribution vanishes due to spin conservation. To elucidate the low-energy description, we derive an effective Hamiltonian projected onto the $s$-orbital subspace using the Schur complement. We show that a naive application of the Kubo formula within this effective model fails to capture the ME response. This issue is resolved by systematically incorporating vertex corrections in terms of orbital hybridization into the response functions. Furthermore, by introducing a quasiparticle renormalization scheme, we formulate a renormalized Kubo formula that preserves conservation laws and accurately reproduces the full multi-orbital results. Our analysis revisits the conventional low-energy perspective and reveals that the ME effect originates from virtual interorbital processes encoded in vertex corrections, rather than from the bare low-energy Hamiltonian. The effective framework developed here provides a unified microscopic understanding of orbital-driven ME responses and offers a systematic route to incorporate hybridization effects beyond simple low-energy models.

</details>

Abstract: Based on first principles calculations, we show in hole-doped La2-xSrxCuO4, the Sr dopants prefer to form dimers, rather than be isolated as generally believed. Same trend applies to other superconductors such as Na-doped Ca2CuO2Cl2, and electron type Nd2-xCexCuO4. The dimer structure makes the two doped holes (electrons) bound up and form a singlet which is also polarized. Another type of dopant in cuprates is oxygen which behaves similarly. So, we propose that, for all oxide high TC superconductors, the holes (electrons) are pre-paired (Z-pairs) during the preparation of the superconductor. The spontaneously formed Z-pairs are conceptually different from not only the Cooper pairs, but also the Zhang-Rice singlets. The attraction force in a Z-pair originates basically from the strong antiferromagnetic exchange energy. We also found that the on-site Coulomb repulsion (U) is no longer a constant due to doping. Around a doping site, U can reduce its value discontinuously, which plays a key role in stabilizing the Z-pairs against isolated holes (electrons). A Z-pair is a p-wave like localized state which breaks the symmetry of the CuO2 plain and attracts each other along the direction perpendicular to the polarization. The Z-pairs may be popular in all of the unconventional superconductors and the periodic assembly can result in the emergence of coherence states and superconductivity.

Abstract: The observation of superconductivity emerging from a spin- and valley-polarized normal state in rhombohedral graphene raises a basic puzzle: intravalley Cooper pairs involve states that are not generically degenerate because of trigonal warping, and therefore lack the particle--particle nesting underlying the conventional Cooper instability. While recent model studies suggest that nontrivial band geometry can strongly promote chiral pairing, how this geometric enhancement competes with realistic band-structure-induced pair breaking remains unclear. Here we study Kohn--Luttinger superconductivity in electron-doped rhombohedral tetralayer graphene using a DFT-fitted multiorbital tight-binding model, retaining the full Bloch-wave-function form factors in both RPA screening and the projected Cooper interaction. We separate the superconducting instability into three contributions: the symmetry-resolved form-factor-dressed interaction, the Cooper-pair phase space determined by the symmetrized pair dispersion, and the energy mismatch generated by trigonal warping. We find that the Bloch form factors strongly favor intravalley pairing, with a leading predominantly $p_x-ip_y$ instability and a substantially larger maximum $T_c$ than the intervalley reference state. At the same time, trigonal-warping-induced mismatch cuts off the Cooper logarithm and confines intravalley superconductivity to a much narrower region of the phase diagram. The dominant instability occurs near pair-Lifshitz transitions and primarily in reconstructed annular or three-lobed Fermi-surface regimes. Our results show that superconductivity in rhombohedral graphene is controlled by the combined effects of quantum-geometry-dressed interactions, pair fermiology, and trigonal-warping-induced pair breaking.

</details>

Abstract: Nonreciprocal charge transport, characterized by direction-dependent electrical response, provides a powerful probe of electronic structure and a platform for rectification. However, its realization is typically governed by intrinsic crystal symmetry, restricting the range of suitable materials. Here, we demonstrate nonreciprocal charge transport in an otherwise centrosymmetric superconductor, FeTe0.7Se0.3 (FTS), through kink boundary-induced strain engineering. The stress relaxation during growth generates a cusp-like kink-band boundary with asymmetric curvature, producing a spatially nonuniform strain field and an effective in-plane polarity that breaks inversion symmetry. Pronounced nonreciprocal transport is observed under out-of-plane magnetic fields, with strong dependence on current and temperature across the superconducting transition, in contrast to the negligible response in flat nanosheets. Our results identify kink boundary-induced symmetry breaking as a general route to engineer nonreciprocal charge transport in centrosymmetric systems.

Abstract: In this paper, we investigate vortex dynamics in periodically width-modulated superconducting channels by numerically solving the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau (TDGL) equations. The channels are designed with sinusoidally varying widths characterized by their average width and modulation amplitude. The motion of driven vortices generates an oscillatory electric field with a fundamental frequency, $f_0$, set by the channel periodicity, together with higher-order harmonics. In contrast to the situation of vortex lattice dynamics in a periodic pinning landscape of circular holes considered by Al Luhaibi et al. [1], we show that the harmonic content of the generated electric field can be effectively controlled through the channel geometry. In particular, appropriately engineered channel profiles strongly suppress higher-order harmonics, yielding an electric-field response that closely approaches a pure sinusoidal waveform. These results demonstrate that geometric modulation offers a viable route to engineering low-harmonicity microwave signals in superconducting devices and may enable cryogenic on-chip waveform generators for controlling superconducting qubits. Such devices could provide a useful building block for scalable transmon-based quantum computing architectures.

Abstract: Magnetic flux trapped in a superconducting circuit shifts the critical currents of nearby Josephson junctions and disturbs the operation of the circuit. The usual remedy is a moat etched through the ground plane to hold the flux away from the logic. We report direct write silicon ion implantation with a focused ion beam as a way to make moats without etching the film, by locally suppressing superconductivity in niobium (Nb). Single implanted lines were written across Nb electrodes in films of three thicknesses over two orders of magnitude in ion fluence, and the transition temperature, resistance, and critical current density of each line were measured. The transition temperature of the line falls with fluence while the surrounding Nb is unchanged, and the critical current density falls by about two orders of magnitude before the line becomes resistive at 4.2 K. The product of the critical current and the resistance of the lines follows a single power law in reduced temperature. This gives an estimate of the critical current of a line at any temperature from its transition temperature and resistance. A time dependent Ginzburg-Landau simulation indicates that an implanted region can capture and hold a vortex. From these results we suggest a starting ion energy and fluence for implanted moats in a 100 nm Nb ground plane at 4.2 K and estimate the equivalent broad beam fluence.

Abstract: Spatial interference can break the inversion symmetry of an effective Josephson potential even in a laterally homogeneous junction with inversion-symmetric local energy. We derive a general criterion based on harmonic-dependent complex spatial form factors: a single local harmonic remains a shifted cosine, whereas multiple harmonics can acquire phases incompatible with any common inversion center, permitting cubic interactions and three-wave mixing. Phase reflection reverses all odd-order expansion coefficients about corresponding minima while preserving the even-order ones. For a capacitively shunted junction at fixed charging energy and arbitrary offset charge, it also preserves the complete quantum spectrum, enabling reversal of the odd nonlinear sector without spectral retuning. A centered cross junction with multiple local harmonics is one concrete example. Within the imposed-field model, a three-component magnetic field can generate an inversion-asymmetric effective potential, and reversing an odd number of field components exactly reflects it without changing the quantum spectrum of the capacitively shunted junction.

Abstract: Unconventional superconductivity in correlated systems often appears in the vicinity of a magnetically ordered state. This situation is also relevant for altermagnets, characterized by a nonrelativistic spin-split electronic structure in the presence of Néel order. Many proposed altermagnetic candidates, however, never cross the magnetic instability. We study superconductivity arising from altermagnetic fluctuations in such systems and show that the sublattice polarization (SLP) of the normal-state bands plays a pivotal role in determining the superconducting state. SLP, an inherent property of altermagnetic band structures that persists even without magnetic order, prevents Cooper pairs from forming between different sublattices. This suppresses pairing mediated by the nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic exchange, which favors spin-singlet superconductivity, and leaves longer-range same-sublattice bonds dominant. When the next-nearest-neighbor exchange is ferromagnetic, as expected for frustration-free Néel order, this results in spin-triplet superconductivity. This mechanism is fundamentally different from that in altermagnets with long-range Néel order, where the spin-split Fermi surfaces favor equal-spin pairing. Finally, when longer-range interactions in the disordered phase instead favor singlet pairing, SLP leads to singlet states with characteristic properties distinct from nearest-neighbor pairing states.

</details>

Abstract: Smart insulation is a turn-to-turn layer that insulates in normal operation and conducts only near a hot spot. It could combine the fast ramping of insulated coils with the passive energy dump of non-insulated coils. Metal-insulator transition (MIT) oxides are natural candidates, but the only one tested in a coil so far, V2O3, switches at 150-170 K, far above the 30-70 K window useful for REBCO magnets. We explore the vanadium-oxygen system to lower the transition. TGA redox processing produced multi-phase powders with an effective transition near 127 K, about 25 K below V2O3. Hydrothermal V3O7, hydrated or annealed, was far too resistive and showed no usable transition. V2O3-epoxy specimens provided a robust reference and an acceptance criterion. Cryogenic measurements revealed a reproducible thermal hysteresis, and the measured specific heat of V2O3 corrects earlier model inputs by close to two orders of magnitude. A first electro-thermal model reproduces current sharing through the smart-insulation layer. Doped V2O3 and V8O15 are identified as the next targets.

Abstract: In first-principles calculations of metallic systems, the discrete electronic mesh in the Brillouin zone necessitates a smearing function and its width (e.g., the widely used 0.02 Ry Methfessel-Paxton scheme) to enforce continuity of energy and occupation at the Fermi surface. However, the quantitative impact of such empirical parameters on phonon-mediated superconductivity has long been overlooked, leading to their habitual selection by mere convention. Here, using hexahydride CsH6 as a benchmark, we systematically investigate the magnitude and propagation mechanism of smearing-induced errors against the parameter-free optimized tetrahedron method. Long-tailed smearing delocalizes the occupation window, causing substantial Fermi-level shifts, flattening of the density of states, and hardening phonon modes, thereby weakening electron-phonon coupling. Conversely, short-tailed smearing suffers from insufficient sampling, producing density-of-states oscillations and enhanced Fermi-surface nesting; via amplified electronic screening and Peierls instabilities, this softens acoustic modes and generates imaginary frequencies, causing CsH6 to be dynamically unstable and discarded. However, the optimized tetrahedron method reveals that CsH6 is dynamically stable under ambient pressure with a remarkable superconducting transition temperature of 121 K. To prevent subjective empirical parameters from obscuring theoretically promising high-temperature superconductors, we derive a simple scaling relation for the smearing width, applicable to different smearing functions, from the free-electron-gas model, offering an objective, precise, and efficient protocol for parameter selection in high-throughput screening.

</details>

Abstract: Interdependent networks can be highly fragile because dependency links amplify local failures into cascades that trigger global collapse. Much of the theoretical understanding comes from geometric network models, where dependency range is imposed externally and coupling strength is distance independent, and it is unclear how these insights apply to real materials, where interdependence arises from physical transport. Here we study thermally coupled amorphous superconducting networks, in which dependencies emerge from current redistribution and heat-mediated interactions governed by thermal diffusivity. However, because heat-driven dependency simultaneously broadens and weakens with distance, long-range thermal coupling can stabilize the superconducting phase upon heating, in contrast with its destabilizing role in geometric models. Cascades are anisotropic, with one-dimensional front propagation. These results offer an experimentally testable perspective complementing the geometric framework.

Abstract: Rapid advances in AI have opened a new era for physics, enabling unprecedented capabilities in research and discovery. However, these advances also bring challenges in verifying, trusting, and reusing the results they so rapidly produce. Formalization is a necessary part of the solution to these challenges. In this paper, as well as giving an overview of the role of AI and formalization in physics, we make the case that: (1) formalization is an essential layer for AI-driven discoveries in both computational and theoretical physics; (2) AI is lowering the barrier to, and increasing the efficiency of, formalization in physics, though it remains weaker at choosing definitions and designing libraries than at writing proofs; (3) the speed AI brings to formalization in physics currently comes at the cost of trust, credit, and community; (4) shared, community-reviewed formal libraries such as Physlib should be central in making progress in this field, both as a trusted, reusable foundation for physics results and as the environment in which AI systems can reason about physics; (5) formalizing physics raises challenges beyond those in mathematics, most importantly that a checked proof does not establish that its definitions faithfully capture the intended physics. We also give our perspectives on near-term and long-term directions in these areas.

Abstract: Global monopoles can carry electric charge even when the scalar field that forms them is neutral. We show that a CP-odd operator in the Goldstone effective theory, typically discarded as a total derivative, gives them an electric charge $Q=-Cn$, with $C$ its Wilson coefficient and $n$ the winding number. This is a Witten effect on a global defect, generated by a mixed $θ$-term between electromagnetism and a composite connection built from the Goldstone fields. The same group theory condition that allows the operator also guarantees the existence of monopoles. We also give a fermionic UV completion and determine the coefficient by matching the Goldstone-Wilczek current.

Abstract: The Roy equations for the $S$ and $P$ waves are solved for pion
  masses below the physical value in the region between threshold and
  $s_A=0.84\GeV^2$. The analysis is based on the assumption that the
  phenomenological input above $s_A$ can be used essentially
  unchanged. Moreover, for $M_π\le M_{π,\mathrm{phys}}$, the chiral
  perturbation theory predictions for the two $S$-wave scattering lengths
  provide a very reliable additional input to the Roy equations. The
  outcome is an accurate description of the $S$ and $P$ partial waves as
  the light-quark masses decrease towards the chiral limit. As a test of
  the approach, we evaluate the first few threshold parameters of the $S$,
  $P$, and $D$ waves via Wanders' sum rules and find excellent agreement
  with chiral perturbation theory across the entire range $0 \le M_π\le
  M_{π,\mathrm{phys}}$. We further solve the Roy equations for pion masses
  above the physical value (up to $240\MeV$) and show that the resulting
  solutions agree well with existing lattice data at $M_π=239\MeV$. The
  present work paves the way for analyses of lattice data that fully
  incorporate the constraints of analyticity, unitarity, and crossing
  symmetry, and provides the basis for an accurate study of the dependence
  of the $σ$ and $ρ$ resonances on the light-quark masses, both of
  which will be addressed in future work.

Abstract: The cosmic imbalance of particles over antiparticles may be inherited by the dark matter in a certain class of asymmetric dark matter models. In this article, we thoroughly investigate the signatures of the minimal realization of this idea. In this model a new (stable) inert Higgs, $H'$, plays the role of the mediator that facilitates the asymmetry transfer from the visible to the dark sector. It also constitutes a small dark matter sub-component, while the dominant component stems from a singlet scalar asymmetry. A combination of phenomenological and cosmological constraints requires this new inert Higgs doublet to be quite light $46\,{\rm GeV}<m_{H'}< 580\,{\rm GeV}$ and its spectrum rather compressed, $ΔM = m_{H^\pm}-m_{H_0} \lesssim 1\,{\rm GeV}$ and $δm = m_{A_0}-m_{H_0}\lesssim 5\,{\rm MeV}$. Therefore its charged component can be relatively long-lived on collider length-scales. By recasting searches for compressed Higgsinos at the LHC, we derive new limits on this particle and show that, while at present most of the parameter space for this state is still unconstrained, large and key regions of parameter space will be tested at the HL-LHC by a combination of searches for disappearing charged tracks and displaced soft tracks. Quite remarkably, the lifetime of the charged component of the doublet can be correlated with the dark matter interaction rate at direct detection experiments and we show that the next generation of direct detection experiments has a unique sensitivity to the parameter space in the model. The fact that a small neutral mass splitting is a prediction of the asymmetric setup makes it an interesting candidate to account for the high energy nuclear recoil event recently reported by the LZ experiment. More generally, our detailed phenomenological study holds for any dark matter model involving an inert Higgs doublet with mass below 580 GeV.

</details>

Abstract: The $248$ keV nuclear-recoil candidate reported by LUX-ZEPLIN (LZ) motivates an inelastic dark matter (DM) interpretation, in which scattering converts the DM particle into a slightly heavier state. We provide a comprehensive study of this interpretation in an anomaly-free model of gauged baryon number, where a new vector boson, $Z_B$, mediates interactions between DM and quarks. A representative thermal solution has $m_χ=1$ TeV, $M_{Z_B}=1.44$ TeV, $g_B\simeq0.228$, and a mass splitting of $300$ keV. A small electroweak mixing allows the heavier DM state to decay rapidly, suppressing unwanted signals from its surviving population. This interpretation can be tested through complementary measurements: additional LZ and XENONnT data can establish whether the high-energy recoil signal persists, ATLAS and CMS Run-3 dijet searches can probe the mediator mass and coupling, and a dedicated IceCube analysis of four-quark final states can test DM capture and annihilation in the Sun. Together, these measurements can test complementary parts of the surviving region and assess whether the LZ candidate is consistent with this interpretation.

We present a formulation of the factorisation theorem which is valid beyond the leading order (LO). On this basis, we explicitly demonstrate the cancellation of all $1/\varepsilon$ singularities between the hard, hard-collinear, anti-hard-collinear and soft contributions. For this purpose, a central result of this work is the computation of the $u$-integrated massless two-loop anti-hard-collinear jet function, which determines the structure of the $1/\varepsilon$ singularities. In addition, we compute the hard function at the required order, combine our results with the hard-collinear jet function known from the literature, and employ the shape function whose renormalisation was determined in our previous work. There, we showed, for inclusive as well as for exclusive modes, that certain contributions to these multi-light-cone soft functions are physically irrelevant and can be consistently omitted; the explicit cancellation of the corresponding divergences provides a non-trivial confirmation of this statement. Overall, our findings constitute a non-trivial consistency check of the factorisation framework for this subleading resolved contribution at NLO and provide the basis for a full phenomenological analysis.

Abstract: Most of the mass of the visible universe originates from the strong interaction. Quantitatively reproducing the origin of the nucleon mass is one of the important challenges in fundamental science. Since the next-generation Electron-Ion Collider has identified resolving this problem as one of its major goals, further theoretical studies are needed to develop approaches to investigating the origin experimentally. Abstract: We present BSMScanner, a framework in which a beyond-the-Standard-Model (BSM) parameter scan is specified as a set of declarative YAML documents rather than written as a program. The user declares the free parameters and their priors, the mass matrices and the diagonalisations that act on them, the observables, the theory-validity conditions and the likelihood terms. The framework validates this specification, resolves it into a dependency graph and compiles it once into a typed C++ evaluation kernel, which is then driven by an independently chosen scanning engine: uniform sampling, two differential-evolution engines, or a staged basin-discovery orchestrator, optionally followed by Markov-chain posterior refinement. Flavour physics matrix diagonalisation, PMNS and CKM construction in fixed conventions, tabulated oscillation likelihoods and a library of common flavour observables are provided by the framework and imported by name, so that a model file contains only model-specific definitions; quantities without a closed form, such as a relic density, enter through a C++ or Python plugin interface. We describe the specification schema, the observable and likelihood library, the scanning engines and their configuration, and demonstrate the framework on six benchmark models with $4$ to $26$ free parameters, giving for each the specification, the scan configuration, the resulting best fit and analytic cross-checks of the result.

</details>

Abstract: An eV-scale sterile Majorana neutrino can suppress neutrinoless double-beta decay through destructive interference with the active-neutrino contribution, weakening the resulting constraints on sterile-neutrino parameters. Abstract: We study the entanglement generated by Newtonian self-gravity in a two-particle system of mixed neutrinos and quantify it by means of the concurrence. Starting from the gravitational coupling between the mass operators, we show that the two-particle Hamiltonian splits into local single-particle terms and an effective Ising interaction in the mass basis, the latter being solely responsible for the generation of correlations. For initially separable flavor states the concurrence is obtained in closed analytic form: it oscillates between zero and a maximum fixed by the mixing angle alone, while the gravitational coupling sets the recurrence time. We further establish a one-to-one correspondence between the concurrence and the reduced-state purity previously derived for this model, thus providing an independent quantum-information description of the same gravitationally induced correlations. Finally, we show that the concurrence does not depend on the Majorana phase, so that Dirac and Majorana parametrizations yield identical entanglement dynamics. These results isolate the genuinely entangling sector of the gravitational evolution and provide a compact analytic characterization of gravity-induced quantum correlations in mixed neutrino systems.

Abstract: We revisit the scenario of critical Higgs inflation, in which inflation is driven by the Standard Model Higgs boson with parameters chosen close to a critical point, giving rise to an inflection point in its potential. After reviewing this model, we perform a systematic study of its viability centered around three criteria: (1) consistency with electroweak-scale measurements, (2) ability to reproduce the cosmic microwave background (CMB) observables, and (3) sensitivity of the model predictions to unknown UV physics, based on a breakdown of perturbative unitarity. We first show that compliance with electroweak-scale measurements is controlled by the parameters in the Higgs potential at inflationary scales, also updating the literature values. We then demonstrate how for any choice of potential parameters, agreement with the CMB observables can be achieved through the choice of the nonminimal coupling, in both metric and Palatini formulations of gravity. However, we also find that there exist no regions of parameter space, in either the metric or Palatini formulations, in which compliance with all three physical criteria is possible. While this does not exclude the possibility of the Higgs field driving inflation, it means that predictions for any observable are in principle sensitive to the details of the model's UV completion.

Abstract: Every physical prediction of an event generator is a statement about the hadronic fluxes that can be recorded by particle detectors. By contrast, the formal backbone of modern event-generator precision is provided by the unobservable partonic final states of the parton shower, with hadronisation included as a model. We construct the probability distribution of the hadronic fluxes non-perturbatively, directly from detector operators acting on asymptotic hadronic states, and encode it in an event generator source functional. This distribution contains every measurement a detector can make, and is therefore what a generator must compute with precision, including the effects of hadronisation. Restricting the functional to the energy flow yields the parton shower source functional, which is infrared and collinear safe and contains every calorimetric observable, making it a natural object against which to assess parton-shower accuracy. We compute its logarithmic structure at one loop in $e^+e^-$ annihilation, finding it directly sensitive to kinematic recoil and hence to the recoil scheme of a parton shower. The parton shower functional depends on a continuum of low scales, one for each resolved direction, and in a toy model its logarithms cannot be entirely resummed within a factorisation whose evolution derives only from the high-scale physics. Our operator-based formulation opens the door to the study of event-generator-like systems in theories beyond QCD, including conformal theories such as $N=4$ super-Yang-Mills theory, and thereby offers a new route to non-perturbative insight into event generation.

</details>
