Abstract: In a neutrino background, the vacuum two-neutrino exchange potential $V \propto G_F^2 /r^5$ acts as a much longer-ranged interaction, scaling as $V\sim1/r$ for a directional background. A proto-neutron star (PNS) hosts one of the densest astrophysical neutrino environments known, and naturally provides a setting in which to test whether this pairwise enhancement produces a macroscopic Hartree contribution. We show that this does not happen. In the spin-independent sector, averaging over pair orientations projects the background onto its angular monopole. The bulk Hartree contribution then saturates instead of growing with system size. We also find that the spin-dependent parity-conserving and parity-violating terms do not generate macroscopic growth under the PNS mean-field conditions considered here. Thus, although the pair potential can be strongly enhanced, the resulting bulk interaction remains very small. The suppression comes from the angular and oscillatory structure of the kernel, not from screening. Within the Standard Model mechanism considered here, the effect is not important for the PNS equation of state or for events like pulsar kicks.

Abstract: Lorentz symmetry and the three-prong structure of hadronic top decays provide physical guidance for designing compact quantum jet taggers. We introduce QGrAT (Quantum Grade-Aware Transformer), a quantum transformer that incorporates these structures into its interactions between constituent qubits. Geometric features constructed from the constituent four-momenta are organised by the grades of the spacetime Clifford algebra $C\ell(1,3)$, and their grade-specific inner products determine the pairwise attention couplings. For fixed constituents and co-transforming reference vectors, these couplings are Lorentz invariant, although the component-wise input encoding makes the complete classifier frame dependent. An auxiliary qubit introduces an explicit three-particle interaction through products of pairwise Minkowski inner products, while a trainable in-circuit readout converts the resulting quantum state into a classification score. We evaluate the architecture in state-vector simulation on the Top Tagging benchmarks. With ten constituents per jet, the configuration using grades $0\text{-}3$ and the three-particle ancilla requires twelve qubits and $144$ trainable parameters, reaching AUC $0.944 \pm 0.002$ and background rejection $1/\varepsilon_B = 35.7 \pm 2.4\ (96.8 \pm 7.7)$ at signal efficiency $\varepsilon_S = 0.50\ (0.30)$. This performance is competitive with the L-GATr-Slim baseline at matched constituent and parameter budgets. Adding the three-particle ancilla raises background rejection at $50\%$ signal efficiency from $19.9$ to $35.7$, whereas extending the representation to the tested grade-$4$ feature gives no consistent improvement. The study establishes a framework for incorporating spacetime geometry into quantum attention and identifies explicit three-particle correlations as the principal source of improved background rejection among the architectural variations tested.

</details>

Abstract: Chiral perturbation theory is a power tool for studying the low-energy strong interactions. In the conventional chiral expansion, according to the power counting rule, the higher-order corrections for physical observables are expected to decrease order by order as the chiral dimension increases. However, most previous study of $πK$ scattering lengths by different references showed that the $\mathcal{O}(p^6)$ correction to the scattering lengths is anomalously large and even exceeds the $\mathcal{O}(p^4)$ one. It poses a serious challenge to the convergence of chiral expansion. In order to explore the underlying origin of this phenomenon, the contributions of two-loop diagrams, one-loop diagrams and tree diagrams for the $πK$ scattering amplitude are detailed analyzed. The reducible two-loop integrals, sunset integrals and vertex integrals from the $\mathcal{O}(p^6)$ order all violate the power counting rule. The sunset and vertex integrals can be largely canceled numerically, but not enough. Furthermore, $πK$ scattering is compared with $ππ$ scattering, which seems not violate the power counting rule. The analysis indicates that $ππ$ scattering contains a higher symmetry, although each $\mathcal{O}(p^6)$-order part also violates the power counting rule, but the violation is much smaller than the one from $πK$ scattering. Large kaon mass also makes a contribution to the power-counting breaking, but it is not a major origin. Hence, it needs to pay more attention to the many-loop low-symmetry diagrams in future work. These diagrams may give large contribution and cannot be ignored.

</details>

Abstract: We calculate analytic expression for the cross section up to order $(eB)^2$ for the production of a heavy quark $(Q)$ of mass $m_Q$ through the gluon $(g)$ fusion process $g+g\to Q+\bar{Q}$ in the presence of a constant weak external magnetic field $(B)$ with strength $eB \ll m_Q^2$. This is done using the weak-field expansion of the fermion propagator calculated within Schwinger's proper-time formalism. Owing to the hierarchy of scales ($m_Q^2 \gg eB$), we neglect the effects of the magnetic field on the quark and antiquark spinors in the external lines. Since the magnetic field breaks rotational invariance, the cross section becomes anisotropic and depends on the relative orientation angle $(θ)$ between the magnetic field and the incoming gluons in the center-of-mass frame. The lowest-order magnetic correction to the vacuum cross section scales as $(eB)^2$ and is found to have $\cos 2θ$ and $\cos 4θ$ dependence, reflecting the breaking of rotational symmetry by the magnetic field. The cross section is maximized when the gluons propagate along the field direction ($θ= 0, π$) and minimized in the perpendicular configuration ($θ= π/2$). The resulting asymmetry peaks near the production threshold and falls with increasing center-of-mass energy, scaling as $(eB)^2/m_Q^4$ with the quark mass. These results provide direct, calculable signatures of the initial-stage magnetic field in relativistic heavy-ion collisions which can, in principle, be imprinted on heavy-flavour observables measured in the final state of heavy-ion collisions.

Abstract: We investigate dilepton production via virtual quark bremsstrahlung in a hot, magnetized quark-gluon plasma (QGP), extending the Landau-Pomeranchuk-Migdal (LPM) framework to account for a background magnetic field and for Fermi-Dirac quark statistics. An external electromagnetic field drives the quark distribution out of equilibrium, and this off-equilibrium correction, obtained from the Boltzmann equation in the relaxation-time approximation, is in principle explicit in the electric and magnetic field strengths. We argue that this correction, being linear in the electric field, vanishes identically upon integration over the full dilepton solid angle and therefore does not contribute to the dilepton production rate (DPR). The leading magnetic-field signature in the rate instead enters entirely through the equilibrium sector: the medium density and Debye screening mass, computed from the Landau-level structure of the magnetized QGP, modifying the LPM suppression parameter. Working in the regime relevant to the hydrodynamic phase of heavy-ion collisions, we present the LPM-induced DPR for quark scatterers and establish, for the first time, a nonzero magnetic-field imprint on this observable through the LPM mechanism.

Abstract: Compression can change thermal conductivity differently along polymer chains and across them by altering phonon velocities and lifetimes. At 100, 200, and 300 K, the chain-axis conductivity decreases at every pressure step. Between 3.452 and 5.056 GPa, both transverse components recover: at 300 K they increase by 23.7% and 13.7%, while the longitudinal conductivity decreases by 14.5%. An exact factorization separates the conductivity into a heat-capacity--velocity factor and a weighted effective lifetime. The transverse recovery follows from larger heat-capacity-weighted squared velocities, consistent with stiffer interchain restoring forces. Along the chains, shorter effective lifetimes dominate, and both conductivity-weighted Normal and Umklapp rates increase at 300 K. Comparing the same cubic force-constant blocks at all three pressures, we find that interchain coupling is already substantial at 1.503 GPa and increases under compression.

Abstract: Artificial neural networks (ANN) implemented on crossbar arrays of non-volatile synaptic devices are known to consume much less energy compared to that implemented on conventional computing units. On the contrary, most prior experimental reports (including reports both on solution-processing-based and sputtering-based RRAM devices) require forming to exhibit non-volatility. Avoiding forming reduces variability, eliminates the need for high voltage pulses, and makes the crossbar array design simpler. Further, we confirm the presence of oxygen vacancies in our materials stack using X-ray photo-electron spectroscopy (XPS). Abstract: Accurately describing chemical bond breaking requires capturing strong/static electron correlation, a major challenge for single-reference self-consistent-field methods such as Hartree--Fock and Kohn--Sham density-functional theory (DFT). Fractional occupations substantially improve this situation, as demonstrated by information-density-matrix-functional theory (i-DMFT) and thermally-assisted-occupation DFT (TAO-DFT). Although useful, they are formulated within rather different theoretical frameworks, are somewhat semi-empirical, and their relationship remains unclear. Here we introduce finite-temperature adiabatic-connection reduced density-matrix functional theory (FT-AC-RDMFT), in which i-DMFT and TAO-DFT emerge as different reductions of a unified framework. The effective eigenvalue equation for natural orbitals and the Fermi--Dirac occupation numbers follow directly from the variational principle, while a thermal adiabatic connection relates the empirical entropy coefficient of i-DMFT to a characteristic temperature of the auxiliary non-interacting system. Within this framework, i-DMFT is the exact-exchange-based reduction, whereas TAO-DFT results when exact exchange is replaced by a density-based exchange-correlation functional. Numerical calculations confirm that i-DMFT, apart from a constant, coincides with the exact-exchange realization of the framework, and show that the improved dissociation behavior from finite-temperature occupations extends beyond exact exchange to hybrid and semilocal density functionals. For strongly correlated V2O3, finite-temperature occupations substantially reduce the severely overestimated non-magnetic LDA bulk modulus, bringing it close to experiment. These results establish FT-AC-RDMFT as a common theoretical foundation for finite-temperature occupation-based electronic-structure methods in molecular and solid-state systems.

</details>

Abstract: We develop a fluctuation based Monte Carlo method for evaluating thermal conductivity within the phonon Boltzmann transport equation (BTE) framework. Starting from the energy based variance reduced formulation of the BTE, we derive a relation between the spatial spreading of deviational phonon energy and the macroscopic heat conduction equation. The resulting formulation expresses thermal conductivity in terms of the mean square displacement of deviational phonon particles, without imposing thermal reservoirs. Consequently, thermal conductivity along multiple directions can be evaluated from a single simulation. The method is demonstrated for crystalline silicon at room temperature in bulk, square nanowires, and anisotropic polycrystalline nanostructures. The calculated thermal conductivities agree with theoretical predictions, including the size dependence induced by phonon boundary scattering and the mode-resolved contributions. For anisotropic polycrystalline structures, the method simultaneously resolves the directional thermal conductivities and captures their dependence on grain morphology. These results demonstrate the validity of the proposed fluctuation-based approach for evaluating thermal conductivity across different transport regimes and structural configurations.

For Gaussian measurement errors, the update recovers classical Levenberg--Marquardt refinement while avoiding dense coordinate-space factorization. The same computational framework accommodates correlated Gaussian errors and robust likelihood-based updates. This approach combines likelihood-aware collective updates with matrix-free backpropagation for direct second-order refinement of large atomistic supercells.

Finally, using the GW/BSE data, we calculated a creation energy of ~3.50 eV/pair. We find that the marked improvement in the accuracy of the calculated creation energy, when using the GW/BSE approach, is primarily attributable to the slight decrease in the magnitude of the electron energy-loss function when using the GW/BSE approach for energies below ~10-15 eV. Overall, it is clear that the competition between phonon emission and ionization near the intersection of the phonon scattering and ionization rates is largely responsible for determining the value of the creation energy, with the other major factor being the location and shape of the plasmon peak in the energy-loss function.

Abstract: Magnetic racetrack memory requires localized carriers that combine rapid motion, resistance to perturbations and a detectable signal. Abstract: Condensed matter stands to gain profoundly from widespread adoption of artificial intelligence (AI). However, its current means of data organization impedes this endeavor. As a premier measure of electronic structure underlying material properties, Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) has accumulated over four decades of spectra, yet the community's publication-centered workflow preserves only a highly selected fraction of its experimental record. A shared data ecosystem could transform these fragmented measurements and their contexts into a persistent, collective scientific memory, creating immediate value for reproducibility and reuse while laying the foundation for AI-enabled discovery. In this Perspective, we report outcomes of a recent community workshop and propose a practical path toward such an ecosystem. Its benefits unfold along three timescales. In the near term, standardized, searchable, and citable datasets strengthen analysis pipelines independently of AI. In the intermediate future, community-validated tools operating across laboratories could enable broader reuse and increasingly closed-loop automated experiments. In the long term, a diverse corpus of measured electronic spectra could support foundation models for quantum matter. Looking forward, a diverse corpus of measured electronic spectra could support foundation models for quantum matter and new discoveries. We discuss lessons from existing scientific databases, the current state of AI in ARPES, as well as areas of community convergence on data organization, access, credit, governance, and a staged pilot program.

Abstract: Steering candidate generation toward user-defined objectives is central to inverse materials design, yet remains difficult when objectives are black-box, changing, or non-differentiable, as in many simulation- and experiment-derived properties. Generative models offer a solution but typically require retraining or fine-tuning for each new objective, demanding labeled data and substantial compute, or rely on differentiable guidance that is brittle to gradient noise and undefined for many practical targets. We present DiffGA, a retraining-free framework that injects evolutionary search into the sampling trajectory of a retrained diffusion model, evolving partially denoised states under an arbitrary black-box oracle. Because oracle evaluation often dominates campaign cost, we couple DiffGA with an on-the-fly Bayesian surrogate that reuses representations from state-of-the-art machine-learned force fields (MLFFs). A Gaussian process is fit during the campaign on the force fields's graph embeddings and, once it demonstrably ranks an unseen generation, replaces expensive property evaluations under an uncertainty-aware acquisition rule, reducing oracle cost with negligible loss of accuracy. Across three crystal-design tasks using a frozen MatterGen backbone, DiffGA outperforms diffusion-only, compute-matched, purely evolutionary, and fine-tuned baselines in oracle-assessed rates of stable, unique, and novel hits. Rather than proposing scientific compounds, we use density functional theory to quantify MLFF oracle fidelity, showing that it is a faithful proxy in key regimes but systematically biased in others, delineating when proxy-driven generative discovery can be trusted. On a representative sample, DiffGA's top hits survive first principles re-evaluation as physically realistic hits, while the surrogate-accelerated pipeline preserves this fidelity at a fraction of the cost.

Abstract: Where crystal nuclei form in amorphous phase-change materials decides how they switch. We study six successive 4D-STEM scans, with simultaneous HAADF and EDS data, of a magnetron-sputtered Ge-Sb-Te (GST) film, restricted to the GST layer and its interface with the oxide beneath it. Nucleation sites do not differ in composition or number density: the nuclei lie on the same robust HAADF-EDS relation as the matrix, and at fixed HAADF the spread of the summed EDS signal equals its Poisson counting noise (ratio 1.03 +- 0.05). The nuclei are nevertheless crystalline and stand out in the 4D-STEM max-intensity virtual image (median z = 11.0) but not in HAADF (z = 1.3) or EDS (z = 0.3). We classify the film in four ways (HAADF intensity, I(q) phase mapping, PCA of the whole diffraction patterns and a self-supervised SimCLR encoder) and compare the electron pair distribution functions (PDFs) of all classes after calibrating reciprocal space on the Au cap. All bulk classes share one short-range structure (first shell 2.70-2.74 Å); towards the interface the first sharp diffraction peak (FSDP) moves to lower q, and every method isolates a thin interface-edge environment with a shorter or missing first shell. The I(q) classification isolates, in every scan, an interface environment that holds 62-100% of the nucleus pixels (1.2-2.5-fold above depth) and persists when the nucleus pixels are removed before classification. The interface, rather than a distinct composition or density of the nucleating volume, therefore marks where nuclei form. Over 4.7e3 e/Å^2 the HAADF signal falls by 17%, the first shell shortens by 0.03 Å and the FSDP narrows by 9%, apparently ordering the film as it damages it. All analyses used PySTEMLab, an interactive 4D-STEM analysis environment developed for this work.

Theoretical analysis establishes that the enhancement of the local magnetic ordering temperature arises directly from the strengthening of exchange interactions due to the small degree of non-magnetic disorder, whereas $T_{\rm C}$ is hardly influenced by the much larger level of magnetic-atom disorder. The combined experimental and theoretical investigations on the anti-Heusler alloy Al$_2$MnCu reveal and explain the unusual observation of nonmagnetic disorder-induced elevation of $T_{\rm C}$, providing a paradigm for the counterintuitive role of disorder in magnetic materials.

Abstract: Hidden beneath the vanishing charge Hall response of altermagnets are spin-up and spin-down Hall currents of opposite sign---concealed, not absent---whose cancellation is enforced by symmetry. When these spin states are locked to distinct valleys, the hidden currents constitute a spin-valley Hall effect. Here we show that in 2D altermagnets with spin-valley-layer coupling (SVLC), a gate field unveils this hidden spin-valley Hall response.~By lifting the symmetry-enforced compensation between valleys with opposite layer polarizations, the field converts the hidden response into a net charge Hall signal, yielding a gate-switchable electric Hall effect (EHE) enabled by SVLC. Using collinear spin layer group (cSLG) theory, we classify all 158 altermagnetic cSLGs, identify 32 that support SVLC, and predict monolayer Mg$_2$Os$_2$(SO$_5$)$_2$ and the Ca(CoN)$_2$-family as candidate materials. First-principles calculations for Mg$_2$Os$_2$(SO$_5$)$_2$ reveal reversible valley splitting, a gate-switchable Hall response, and a Kerr rotation exceeding 1 mrad whose sign reverses together with the EHE signal. These results establish a symmetry-based route to all-electrical valley-spin control and identify altermagnets as promising platforms for high-performance valley transport and electrically switchable magneto-optics.

Abstract: Magnetic frustration is commonly quantified by the frustration index, calculated from the Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures. Because both quantities are not simultaneously available for most materials, direct prediction of the magnetic frustration index remains challenging. Here, we propose a machine learning workflow for indirect prediction of the frustration index and consequent screening of potential frustrated magnetic compounds through independent prediction of Curie-Weiss and Neel temperatures. In our work, composition-based models implemented via the CatBoost algorithm, trained on experimental data were optimized using chemically informed dataset subdivision. The highest predictive accuracy was achieved for the rare earth-oxide model in predicting Neel and for the rare earth model in predicting Curie-Weiss. Ensemble uncertainty was incorporated through a conservative frustration index to improve the reliability of candidate selection. Screening of approximately 67000 Materials Project compounds identified 20 high-confidence frustrated magnetic candidates, including several experimentally known frustrated magnets and previously unexplored oxide systems potential for further investigation.

Abstract: The anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in magnetic Weyl semimetals arises from distinct microscopic origins-intrinsic Berry curvature and extrinsic scattering mechanisms-yet reversible switching between these regimes within a single material platform remains elusive. The intermediate composition PrAl0.90Si0.94 (Al/Si ~0.96) exhibits high carrier mobility, multi carrier transport, yielding a giant AHC of 1413 Ω-1cm-1 dominated by extrinsic skew-scattering. The Si-poor and Si-rich compositions exhibit lower AHC values and intrinsic Berry-curvature-dominated AHE, establishing a composition-driven intrinsic-extrinsic-intrinsic crossover. Deviating from the intermediate composition suppresses mobility and reverses carrier type from hole-like to electron-like. The Al/Si ratio thus serves as a chemical knob that primarily modulates the Fermi level via compositional band filling, while off-stoichiometry-induced chemical disorder plays a subordinate role in determining the AHE mechanism. These results position off-stoichiometric PrAlSi as a tunable platform where subtle compositional variation reversibly switches the AHE mechanism.

Abstract: Superionic conductors are promising electrolyte candidates for all-solid-state batteries, thanks to their liquid-like ionic mobility. In the prototypical lithium-ion oxide superionic conductor with antifluorite structure, the high-temperature transition to the fast-ion regime has long been attributed to volume-driven harmonic phonon softening. However, this quasi-harmonic picture neglects the strong lattice anharmonicity inherent to the fast-ion regime, leaving the microscopic origin of the transition unresolved. Here, we apply non-perturbative lattice dynamics combined with a first-principles-accurate machine-learning interatomic potential to study the phase transition in Li$_2$O. Finally, we analyze the spectral properties using the time-dependent self-consistent harmonic approximation, revealing the breakdown of the quasi-particle picture in this material. The spectral function and the infrared response provide signtures of the phase transition that can be investigated experimentally using inelastic neutron or X-ray scattering and infrared spectroscopy. All these results establish the extreme anharmonicity of Li$_2$O near the superionic regime and provide a lattice-dynamic framework for understanding and engineering the fast-ion transition in this type of materials.

Abstract: We propose a novel amorphous oxide semiconductor (AOS)-based conductive-semiconducting-conductive (CSC) MOS test structure to effectively sense, discriminate, and analyze distinct trap-induced conductance losses in AOS transistors. In conventional test structures, non-ideal metal-AOS contacts convolute the conductance loss generated by various trap states. The CSC architecture mitigates these contact-related artifacts, enabling the decoupling of interface states and shallow states. Furthermore, the contributions from leakage, series resistance, and device-geometry effects are identified and calibrated. Our results yield bias-resolved trap profiles and reveal three distinct regimes: (1) In the depletion region, interface states dominate the conductance loss. These states exhibit sub-microsecond time constants and their density increases slightly with deeper depletion, which is associated with donor-like states. (2) From weak to moderate accumulation, shallow states become the primary contributor, displaying a Gaussian-like volumetric density-of-states distribution with response times spanning from microseconds to nanoseconds. (3) In the strong accumulation regime, interface states regain dominance with an increased state quantity, while their time constants remain invariant. These bias-resolved profiles clarify how different trap populations impact AOS transistors under distinct operating conditions, providing intuitive metrics for understanding reliability degradation. Consequently, the CSC structure offers a simple, scalable, and robust platform for trap characterization in AOS transistors.

</details>

These results identify interlayer registry as the central control parameter for forming and tuning moir'e polar topology in twisted bilayer h-BN.

Abstract: Designing synthesizable materials with targeted properties remains one of the central objectives in materials sciences. Ionic liquids (ILs) are well suited to this goal due to their "tunability", where physical and chemical properties can be precisely customized by modifying their molecular structure, specifically by varying the combination of cations and anions. At the same time, current data-driven property models for ionic liquids often degrade in scarce, noisy, and uneven data regimes, where measurements cover only a small fraction of chemical space and a limited range of thermodynamic conditions. This makes out-of-distribution screening brittle: even when point predictions look plausible, it is difficult to compare candidates consistently, quantify downstream implications, or trust rankings enough to guide molecular discovery. We present a property-prediction and optimal candidate selection workflow that treats data processing, deep learning modeling, and physics-based calculations as a coupled system, linking property prediction with uncertainty estimation and physics-based filtering. By tightening the link between predicted properties and the criteria used for down-selection, the workflow supports reliable out-of-distribution ranking and enables prioritization of previously untested ionic liquid candidates with favorable predicted trade-offs. We demonstrate the application of our workflow by designing novel ionic liquids for CO2 capture from flue gas, predicting their properties and selecting Pareto-optimal candidates based on CO2 solubility, IL viscosity and working capacity.

Our findings establish the EEE as a symmetry-guided, gate-tunable route to electrical spin manipulation and open avenues for multistate spintronics in 2D quantum materials.

This inversion reflects two coupled microscopic mechanisms: a growing low-frequency gap between the phonon densities of states of the ordered and disordered phases as the hydrogen-bond network densifies, and an increase in molecular symmetry that progressively suppresses orientational disordering. Because the vibrational channel is substantially less sensitive to pressure than the orientational one, hydrogen-bond density emerges as a practical, chemically accessible design handle for tailoring not only the magnitude but also the pressure robustness of the caloric response in hydrogen-bonded plastic crystals, with direct implications for the search for new barocaloric and thermal-energy-storage materials.

Abstract: Photogenerated carriers in lead-halide perovskites are widely believed to move as large polarons, whose accompanying lattice distortion is thought to screen the carrier from charged defects. Three recent findings call this picture into question. First, time-resolved structural probes now measure the photoinduced lattice distortion directly; yet calculations show that thermal motion of the lattice alone can raise the carrier effective mass-an increase previously taken as evidence for polarons-so that it remains unclear whether the lattice distortion around a carrier is really a polaron. Second, the carrier-lattice coupling strengthens as the material is thinned from 3D to 2D. In the two main families of 2D perovskites, Ruddlesden-Popper and Dion-Jacobson, the carrier couples to the lattice through different channels, and their contrasting behavior is set by which coupling channel dominates rather than by lattice stiffness alone. Third, the first direct test of defect tolerance does not point to polaron screening; its authors attribute defect tolerance to the soft, disordered lattice. New transport data show that polaron screening reduces scattering between carriers, but not that it protects carriers from defects. Polaron formation, even if established, therefore does not establish polarons as the cause of defect tolerance. Separating the two requires measuring the lattice distortion as a function of excitation density, over a range spanning the low densities of a solar cell under sunlight and the much higher densities used in most ultrafast experiments. On this basis, we propose experiments that can clarify how photogenerated carriers behave in lead-halide perovskites.

Abstract: Kohn-Sham density-functional theory is expensive because it needs orbitals that extend over the whole system. For insulators this can be avoided by using only the diagonal of the one-electron resolvent, $g(\mathbf{r},z)=\langle\mathbf{r}|(z-\hat h)^{-1}|\mathbf{r}\rangle$, at a few complex energies $z$. We call the scheme local-resolvent density-functional theory (LRes-DFT). The density and the band energy are linear in $g$, so neither orbitals nor a kinetic-energy functional are needed. The complex energies are $M$ equally spaced points on a circle that encloses the occupied levels, offset by half a spacing so that no point lies on the real axis. Summing the resolvent over them is equivalent to occupying each level with $1/(1+u^M)$, where $u$ is the level's energy measured from the circle's center in units of its radius. This is a smooth step that is near 1 inside the circle and near 0 outside, and it stays between 0 and 1 for every $M$. If the circle moves with the chemical potential, forces at fixed electron number are exact. Gauss-Legendre and Zolotarev rules need fewer points when the gap is small. In three dimensions we use eigenpairs of small windows around each molecule instead of resolvents. This is divide-and-conquer (DC) with a different occupation function, and it is as accurate as DC with Fermi-Dirac occupations. For H$_2$ molecular crystals of up to 1024 electrons it agrees with Kohn-Sham to $2.5\times10^{-7}$ Ha per electron. On one core it is slower than dense diagonalization below about 500 electrons and 3.5 times faster at 1024 electrons. It loses accuracy for disordered or compressed systems and for diffuse basis functions.

</details>

Abstract: Deep neural networks that predict density functional theory (DFT) Hamiltonians from material structures offer a route to efficient electronic-structure calculations. In this work, we report a controlled, nonvolatile, but reversible local modification of such a system by electrically driven oxygen migration through the metallic film structure by using ionic liquid gating. Our findings provide direct evidence for the modification of the oxidation state at the heavy metal (HM)/ferromagnet (FM) interface. As a result, we observed changes in fundamental magnetic properties, such as an increase in the effective anisotropy constant (Keff) and coercive field (Hc) with oxidation, and a decrease with the reduction of oxygen ions. Positive gate voltages relative to the thin film extract oxygen ions from the magnetic layer, significantly reducing the domain nucleation field. In contrast, negative voltages drive oxygen ions into the magnetic layer, leading to a reversible increase in the extent of domain wall pinning near saturation. We observe a corresponding decrease in the magnetic moment and DMI, with negative voltage reducing both. This magneto-ionic modulation in fully metallic structures is beneficial for spintronic device applications, particularly in field-programmable domain wall and skyrmion devices.

The structural (X-ray diffraction and scanning electron microscopy), compositional (elastic recoil detection analysis), and optical properties, including photochromic behaviour, of the films are systematically investigated. The results reveal a strong influence of substrate orientation on the film microstructure and crystallographic texture, leading to epitaxial-like growth with a mosaic spread of ~3-4°. YHO deposited on YSZ(100) exhibits larger crystallite sizes (10-20 nm) than YHO grown on YSZ(111) (~8 nm) and exhibits residual in-plane compressive strain while remaining largely relaxed with respect to the substrate. Films deposited at 200 °C exhibit lower oxygen concentrations (O/Y = 0.71-0.82) than films deposited at RT (O/Y = 0.92-1.26). All films exhibit photochromic behaviour under solar simulator illumination, with relative transmittance changes ranging from 7 to 22 % after 10 min of illumination. Despite the pronounced structural differences, the epitaxial-like films show no systematic improvement in photochromic response, indicating that the effect is governed primarily by composition and the local O/H configuration rather than by long-range cation ordering. These findings provide new insights into the relationship between crystallographic texture, composition, and photochromic properties in YHO thin films, contributing to the understanding of this material for optical and optoelectronic applications.

Abstract: Strain gradients, unlike homogeneous strain, are odd under space inversion and unlock couplings that are strictly forbidden in the parent crystal. At the nanoscale, and in curved low-dimensional systems in particular, such gradients are readily attainable and often unavoidable, so that curvature acts as a symmetry-breaking field written into the lattice itself. This chapter reviews the resulting couplings to electric polarization and magnetism. After establishing the geometric relation between strain gradients and curvature, we review flexoelectricity as the better-established reference case: the bulk, surface and dynamic contributions that complicate its definition, its microscopic mechanisms, its manifestation in two-dimensional materials, and the first-principles methodology now available for its calculation. Using the magnetoelectric effect as a conceptual bridge, we then develop flexomagnetism in a broad sense, covering any coupling phenomena between magnetism and strain gradients, and specifically discuss the linear magnetization response to curvature and curvature-induced Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions, beyond the established curvilinear treatment. Finally, we briefly describe some additional effects fitting under this broad umbrella of flexomagnetism, including nonlinear ones, inhomogeneous magnetoelectric and Rashba-type phenomena, and connections to altermagnetism.

</details>

Abstract: Kagome lattices provide a simple geometry for engineering electronic states that exhibit novel quantum behavior. In conventional kagome materials, however, the relevant states are often embedded in chemically complex three-dimensional crystals and are difficult to isolate or tune. Molecule-based kagome networks offer structural precision and tunability, but have largely been confined to metallic substrates incompatible with 2D devices. Here we report growth of single-layer Ni$_3$(HITP)$_2$ metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) on van der Waals substrates including graphene-based field-effect devices using metal-salt molecular beam epitaxy. Low-temperature scanning tunneling spectroscopy resolves kagome-derived flat-band and van Hove singularity-based spectral features consistent with first-principles calculations. Gating hybrid 2D MOF devices allows us to energetically shift kagome spectral features, thus establishing electrostatic control of chemically programmable 2D kagome electronic lattices.

Abstract: Mixed-anion semiconductors offer opportunities for bandgap engineering beyond conventional multication alloys, but competing phases and disorder complicate their synthesis and the identification of their electronic properties. Gallium oxynitride (GaON) could connect the technologically important GaN and Ga2O3 systems, yet establishing a clear relation between its crystal structure, composition and bandgap remains challenging. Here we report the direct growth of crystalline spinel gamma-GaON films by nitrogen-plasma-assisted pulsed-laser deposition. Varying the nitrogen and oxygen supply yields monoclinic beta-Ga2O3, predominantly disordered films and crystalline spinel GaON. Complementary diffraction, atomic-resolution microscopy and elemental analysis establish the spinel structure and nitrogen incorporation. Temperature-dependent photocurrent spectroscopy reveals a clear redshift of the crystalline gamma-GaON response, supporting assignment of a band-edge transition near 4.3 eV at room temperature. These results open opportunities to investigate mixed-anion wide-bandgap semiconductor physics and develop heterostructures.

Abstract: Lattice vibrations govern the propagation of heat in materials, and investigating their dynamics through time-resolved phonon dispersion measurements would provide insight into these excitations and their interactions. However, time-resolved measurements that directly resolve the phonon dispersion at the nanoscale have not been previously demonstrated. Momentum-resolved vibrational electron energy loss spectroscopy (EELS) provides access to phonon energies and momenta with nanoscale spatial resolution. Here, we combine momentum-resolved vibrational EELS with pulsed laser heating and synchronised gated electron detection to observe the phonon dispersion of graphite during controlled heating and cooling on the microsecond timescale. Recording both the gain and loss spectra at defined times relative to laser excitation, we determine the temperature at each time delay and follow changes to the phonon branches as a function of temperature. The momentum-resolved spectra reveal a decrease in energy of the optical phonon branch with increasing temperature accompanied by an increase in peak width. These changes are consistent with temperature-dependent phonon renormalisation and scattering associated with anharmonic interactions. The approach is compatible with detector gating on timescales down to 50 ns, providing a route towards extending these measurements to faster dynamics. This work demonstrates time-resolved phonon dispersion spectroscopy using vibrational EELS and provides a route towards probing temperature-dependent phonon dynamics at the nanoscale with simultaneous energy and momentum resolution.

</details>

Abstract: Double perovskites (DPs) offer a compositionally versatile platform for photocatalysis and optoelectronics, but their soft lattices favor strong exciton-phonon coupling and self-trapped excitons (STEs), which produce broad, red-shifted photoluminescence (PL). Here, we combine optical spectroscopy and ab initio calculations to study lead-free, Sb-doped Cs2BInCl6 nanocrystals with B-site alloying from Na to K. Alloying tunes the PL energy from about 2.48 to 2.70 eV, while absorption features near 3.8 eV remain nearly unchanged. Calculations reveal that Sb doping enables bright radiative recombination channels and delocalizes the excitonic wavefunction. We identify STEs responsible for the large PL red shift, associated with an upward shift of dopant-hybridized in-gap states. Na-to-K substitution selectively changes the STE lattice distortion, explaining why PL varies strongly with composition while absorption does not. Theory further predicts pronounced PL polarization anisotropy that reflects the STE wavefunction's spatial structure and depends on B-site composition. Time-resolved anisotropy measurements closely agree with these predictions, providing experimental fingerprints of the excitonic wavefunction and its evolution with alloying. Together, these results disentangle the roles of doping and alloying in lead-free DPs and establish polarization-resolved spectroscopy as a probe of STE wavefunctions.
